\begin{document}
\begin{center}
{\Large\bfseries The sharp anticanonical volume bound\\[3pt]
for exceptional Fano surfaces\par}
\vspace{12pt}
13 November 2026
\end{center}
\begin{abstract}
We prove that every exceptional complex klt del Pezzo surface has
anticanonical volume at least \(1/31801\). The bound is attained by
Totaro's hypersurface of degree \(354\) in
\(\PP(11,49,118,177)\), and no restriction on the Picard number is imposed.
The proof combines orbifold Riemann--Roch with the
K\"ahler--Einstein inequality, a finite catalogue of quotient
singularities, and the geometry of the reduced support of a first
anticanonical member. In the potentially extremal range, these arguments
force the first anticanonical section to occur in degree eleven.
A rational linear certificate then yields the sharp bound.
The infinite local families are controlled analytically, and the
remaining finite calculations are supplied with exact, solver-free
verification programs. The bound also holds for exceptional klt weak
del Pezzo surfaces. We do not classify the surfaces attaining equality.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

\subsection{The volume problem}

Let \(X\) be a normal projective surface over \(\C\). A \emph{klt del
Pezzo surface}, or a \emph{klt Fano surface}, is a surface with klt
singularities for which \(-K_X\) is ample. It is \emph{exceptional} if
\((X,D)\) is klt for every effective \(\Q\)-divisor
\(D\sim_{\Q}-K_X\). We use the same definition when \(-K_X\) is nef and
big, in which case \(X\) is called a weak del Pezzo surface.

The volumes of arbitrary klt del Pezzo surfaces have no positive
uniform lower bound. Exceptionality changes the problem: it excludes
anticanonical boundaries with a non-klt center, and exceptional Fano
varieties form a bounded family in each dimension \cite{Bir19}.
The numerical question is to determine the optimal positive lower
bound.

Totaro constructed an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface
\[
 X_{354}=\{w^2+z^3+xy^7+x^{17}yz=0\}
       \subset\PP(11,49,118,177),
\]
with \((-K_{X_{354}})^2=1/31801\)
\cite[Theorems 3.1 and 6.1]{Tot24}. His conjecture that the analogous
examples minimize anticanonical volume in every dimension has the
following surface case.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:main}
Let \(X\) be an exceptional complex klt del Pezzo surface. Then
\[
                         (-K_X)^2\geq\frac1{31801}.
\]
The constant is sharp, being attained by \(X_{354}\).
The same inequality holds for exceptional complex klt weak del Pezzo
surfaces.
\end{theorem}

The assertion concerns all Picard numbers. This is essential: the
known extremal example has Picard number two \cite{Tot24}.
Sharpness here means attainment of the optimal numerical constant;
it does not assert uniqueness of the equality surface.

\subsection{Structure of the proof}

Write
\[
 V=K_X^2,\qquad
 P_m=h^0(X,\OO_X(-mK_X)),\qquad
 b=\min\{m\geq1:P_m>0\}.
\]
All divisorial sheaves are interpreted reflexively. The integer \(b\)
is finite because \(-K_X\) is ample. Exceptionality implies \(P_1=0\):
an effective integral anticanonical divisor would have a component
with coefficient at least one.

It is enough to study \(0<V\leq1/31801\).
The classification of Liu--Shokurov first implies
\(\mld(X)>1/11\): every exceptional del Pezzo surface outside this
discrepancy range has volume at least \(1/21385\)
\cite[Theorems 1.9 and 8.8, Table 8]{LS23}.
The remaining proof has three stages.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\arabic*)}]
\item \emph{Focused nonvanishing.}
Exceptionality gives a positive orbifold K\"ahler--Einstein metric.
The resulting Chern number inequality, combined with Noether's formula
and orbifold Riemann--Roch, gives a budget for positive local costs.
The budget bounds all possible quotient singularities.
Exact rational inequalities first give \(b\leq29\), and then
\(V\leq1/31801\) gives \(b\leq11\).
\item \emph{Geometry of a first member.}
For \(M\in|-bK_X|\), the reduced total support on a resolution has a
log canonical model \((T,C)\) satisfying
\[
                  0<(K_T+C)^2\leq(b-1)^2V.
\]
The theorem of Liu--Shao makes this pair plt in the relevant volume
range \cite[Theorem 1.3]{LS26}. The curve \(C\) is smooth and rational,
and \(K_T+pC\sim_{\Q}0\) with \(bp\in\Z\).
Adjunction rules out \(b\leq9\); a local intersection congruence rules
out \(b=10\). Thus \(b=11\).
\item \emph{The sharp numerical inequality.}
A second positive cost, using only \(P_1=\cdots=P_{10}=0\), gives a
catalogue of \(2121\) local types. On this catalogue a rational
combination of section inequalities proves \(V\geq1/31801\).
Its exact coefficients are printed in Appendix~\ref{app:sharp-table}.
\end{enumerate}

These stages have different roles. The first makes a global
nonvanishing assertion; the second uses actual curves; the third
is an inequality in a finite-dimensional real cone. At no point is
a formal numerical basket assumed to be geometrically realizable.
Allowing real, nonnegative local multiplicities only enlarges the
set to which the final inequality applies.

\subsection{Relation to the literature and computational content}

The local-cost strategy is motivated by the treatment of stable
surfaces in Liu--Liu \cite[Section 9]{LL26stable}.
The Fano argument needs two changes: the K\"ahler--Einstein hypothesis
must be supplied by exceptionality, and the local correction for
\(-mK_X\) is the canonical correction in degree \(m+1\).
Both points are made explicit below. The weights used here are
adapted to the discrepancy threshold \(1/11\).

We use the public manuscript \cite{LL26stable} for its presentation of
quotient Riemann--Roch and quotient graphs. Its posting is identified
in the bibliography, including its verification status.
Its main stable-volume theorem is not an input to
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}. The reduced-boundary theorem needed here is
the \(1/42\) theorem of Liu--Shao.

The proof includes two finite local calculations and \(87\) rational
linear certificates: \(86\) contradictions for nonvanishing and one
sharp inequality. Completeness of the local calculations is proved
by estimates for the infinite families. The verification programs use
only integers and rational numbers. They do not call an optimization
solver, consult a stored catalogue, or access a network.

The verification files are part of this manuscript. They are
embedded as attachments in the PDF and included literally in the
single TeX source; compiling that source writes them to the
compilation directory. A separate copy is available in the
\href{\efvurl}{verification archive}. Appendix~\ref{app:verification}
describes the file format, the row conventions, and the exact checks.
The geometric arguments are proved in the body of the paper,
independently of any earlier internal notes.

\section{Global identities for exceptional del Pezzo surfaces}
\label{sec:global}

\subsection{Conventions and vanishing}

For a prime divisor \(E\) over \(X\), \(a(E,X,\Delta)\) denotes its
log discrepancy. Thus a component of \(\Delta\) with coefficient
\(d\) has log discrepancy \(1-d\).
We write \(\mld(X)\) for the infimum of log discrepancies.
A complex klt surface has quotient singularities and is
\(\Q\)-factorial \cite[Chapter 4]{KM98}.

We will use the following standard form of vanishing.
If \(L\) is an integral Weil divisor on a klt projective surface and
\(L-K_X\) is nef and big, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weil-kv}
                         H^i(X,\OO_X(L))=0\quad(i>0).
\end{equation}
This is the Weil-divisor form of Kawamata--Viehweg vanishing;
see \cite[Corollary 2.49]{Fuj09}.
For a del Pezzo surface it applies to \(L=-mK_X\) for every
\(m\geq0\), so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chi-P}
                    P_m=\chi(X,\OO_X(-mK_X)),\qquad\chi(\OO_X)=1.
\end{equation}

The minimal resolution \(\pi:Y\to X\) is rational.
Indeed, quotient singularities are rational, hence
\(q(Y)=p_g(Y)=0\). If
\(\pi^*K_X=K_Y+B_Y\) with \(B_Y\geq0\) exceptional, then
\(K_Y\cdot\pi^*(-K_X)=-V<0\). A nef divisor has nonnegative
intersection with every pseudo-effective divisor, so \(K_Y\) is
not pseudo-effective. The classification of smooth surfaces gives a
ruled minimal model; \(q(Y)=0\) makes its base rational.
Consequently
\[
                       K_Y^2+\rho(Y)=10,\qquad e(Y)=2+\rho(Y).
\]

\subsection{Exceptionality and orbifold Chern numbers}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:orbifold}
If \(X\) is an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface and \(g_x\) is the
order of the local uniformizing group at \(x\in\operatorname{Sing}X\),
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:orbifold}
 V\leq3e_{\rm orb}(X),\qquad
 e_{\rm orb}(X)=e(X)-\sum_x\left(1-\frac1{g_x}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The algebraic alpha invariant is the infimum of
\(\operatorname{lct}(X;D)\) over effective \(D\sim_{\Q}-K_X\).
Exceptionality implies that every such threshold is greater than one,
so the infimum is at least one. The orbifold alpha criterion therefore
applies, since \(1>2/3\), and gives a positive orbifold
K\"ahler--Einstein metric
\cite[Section 6, Criterion 6.4]{DK01}.

For a K\"ahler--Einstein complex surface the Chern--Weil identity
expresses \(3c_2-c_1^2\) as a nonnegative multiple of the squared norm
of the trace-free curvature. The identity is local and hence also
holds in finite quotient charts, with integrals divided by the group
orders. The orbifold Chern numbers are \(c_1^2=K_X^2\) and
\(c_2=e_{\rm orb}(X)\). This proves \eqref{eq:orbifold}.
\end{proof}

Here \(g_x\) is the order of the full local quotient group.
For a noncyclic quotient it need not equal the determinant of the
exceptional intersection matrix. Keeping this distinction is
necessary for the numerical argument.

\subsection{Resolution invariants and Riemann--Roch}

At a singular point \(x\), let \(E_1,\ldots,E_{\ell_x}\) be the curves
in the minimal resolution. Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-invariants}
 \begin{split}
 A_x&=(-E_i\cdot E_j)_{i,j},\qquad
 k_x=(-E_i^2-2)_i,\qquad b_x=A_x^{-1}k_x,\\
 \gamma_x&=\ell_x-k_x^tb_x,\qquad
 \mu_x=\gamma_x+1-\frac1{g_x}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The matrix \(A_x\) is positive definite. Adjunction on the rational
exceptional curves shows that, locally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrepancy}
       \pi^*K_X=K_Y+\sum_i b_{x,i}E_i,\qquad
       a(E_i,X,0)=1-b_{x,i}.
\end{equation}
On a minimal resolution of a quotient singularity,
\(0\leq b_{x,i}<1\), and the minimum of these log discrepancies is
the minimal log discrepancy of the germ.

For \(m\geq0\), define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-correction}
 a_x(m)=\frac12\bigl(k_x^tf-f^tA_xf\bigr),
                  \qquad f=\fracpart{(m+1)b_x},
 \qquad Q_m=\frac{m(m+1)}2.
\end{equation}
Fractional parts are taken coordinatewise.
The function \(a_x(m)\) is periodic with period dividing the local
Cartier index of \(K_X\), and \(a_x(0)=0\).
At canonical singularities it vanishes identically.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:global-identities}
For an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface,
\begin{align}
 P_m&=1+Q_mV+\sum_xa_x(m) \quad(m\geq0),\label{eq:RR}\\
 V&=10-\rho(X)-\sum_x\gamma_x,\label{eq:noether}\\
 \sum_x\mu_x&\leq12-\frac43V.\label{eq:budget-mu}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The quotient Riemann--Roch formula in resolution form gives the
canonical correction
\(\frac12(k_x^t\{lb_x\}-\{lb_x\}^tA_x\{lb_x\})\) in degree \(l\);
see \cite[Proposition 5.2]{Bla95} and
\cite[Definition 9.2 and Lemma 9.4]{LL26stable}.
Serre duality for the maximal Cohen--Macaulay divisorial sheaf
\(\OO_X(-mK_X)\) gives
\(\chi(-mK_X)=\chi((m+1)K_X)\).
Thus the required degree is \(l=m+1\).
Combining the formula with \eqref{eq:chi-P} proves \eqref{eq:RR}.
For \(m=0\), the equality \(k_x^tb_x=b_x^tA_xb_x\) verifies
\(a_x(0)=0\) directly.

By \eqref{eq:discrepancy},
\[
 K_Y^2=V-\sum_x k_x^tb_x,\qquad
 \rho(Y)=\rho(X)+\sum_x\ell_x.
\]
Rational Noether gives \eqref{eq:noether}.
Each exceptional tree has Euler number \(\ell_x+1\), so contracting it
changes the Euler number by \(\ell_x\). Therefore
\(e(X)=2+\rho(X)\), and
\[
 e_{\rm orb}(X)
 =2+\rho(X)-\sum_x(1-1/g_x)
 =12-V-\sum_x\mu_x.
\]
Now \eqref{eq:orbifold} gives \eqref{eq:budget-mu}.
\end{proof}

The elimination of \(\rho(X)\) in \eqref{eq:budget-mu} is the reason
the local-cost method works without a Picard-rank hypothesis.

\section{Local calculations for quotient singularities}\label{sec:local}

We record the local formulas in detail, including the estimates that
make the later finite enumeration exhaustive. The classification of
quotient surface singularities into chains and spherical forks is
standard; see \cite[Theorem 4.7]{KM98} and
\cite[Definition 2.9 and Lemma 2.10]{LL26stable}.

\subsection{Cyclic chains}

Let \(x\) be of type \(\frac1r(1,q)\), where
\(r\geq2\), \(1\leq q<r\), and \(\gcd(r,q)=1\).
Its minimal resolution has Hirzebruch--Jung chain
\[
 \frac rq=[e_1,\ldots,e_\ell]
       =e_1-\frac1{e_2-\frac1{\cdots-\frac1{e_\ell}}},
                   \qquad e_i\geq2.
\]
Write \(q'\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}\) for the inverse of \(q\) modulo \(r\).
Reversing the chain exchanges \(q\) and \(q'\).
Put \(\alpha_i=1-b_i\), with boundary values
\(\alpha_0=\alpha_{\ell+1}=1\). The chain equations are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-recurrence}
 e_i\alpha_i=\alpha_{i-1}+\alpha_{i+1},\qquad
 \alpha_1=\frac{q+1}{r},\qquad
 \alpha_\ell=\frac{q'+1}{r}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cyclic-identities}
Let \(B=\sum_i(e_i-2)\). Then
\begin{align}
 \sum_i(e_i-2)\alpha_i&=2-\alpha_1-\alpha_\ell,\label{eq:chain-sum}\\
 \mu_x&=\ell-B+3-\frac{q+q'+3}{r},\label{eq:mu-cyclic}\\
 r&=\frac1{\alpha_1}
       +\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\frac1{\alpha_i\alpha_{i+1}}
       +\frac1{\alpha_\ell}.\label{eq:det-identity}
\end{align}
If \(\mld(x)>1/11\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cyclic-rough}
               B\leq19,\qquad
               \mu_x>\ell-\frac{37}{2},\qquad
               r<121\ell-99.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Summing \eqref{eq:chain-recurrence} gives
\eqref{eq:chain-sum}. Hence
\[
 k^tb=B-\sum_i(e_i-2)\alpha_i
     =B-2+\alpha_1+\alpha_\ell.
\]
Substitution in \eqref{eq:local-invariants}, with \(g_x=r\), gives
\eqref{eq:mu-cyclic}.

For \eqref{eq:det-identity}, let \(z_0=0,z_1=1\), and
\(z_{i+1}=e_i z_i-z_{i-1}\). The continuant recurrence gives
\(z_{\ell+1}=r\).
The discrete Wronskian
\(z_{i+1}\alpha_i-z_i\alpha_{i+1}\) is constant in \(i\);
at \(i=0\) it is one. Therefore
\[
 \frac{z_{i+1}}{\alpha_{i+1}}-\frac{z_i}{\alpha_i}
                         =\frac1{\alpha_i\alpha_{i+1}}.
\]
Summing for \(i=0,\ldots,\ell\) proves the determinant identity.

If all \(\alpha_i>1/11\), \eqref{eq:chain-sum} implies
\(B/11<20/11\), hence \(B\leq19\).
Since \(0\leq b_i<1\), one has \(k^tb<19\) when \(B>0\);
the assertion is also immediate when \(B=0\). Thus
\(\mu_x=\ell-k^tb+1-1/r>\ell-37/2\).
The two end terms in \eqref{eq:det-identity} are each less than
eleven, and each of the \(\ell-1\) interior terms is less than \(121\).
This gives \(r<22+121(\ell-1)\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{A floor-sum expression for the correction}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cyclic-correction}
For a cyclic quotient as above and \(m\geq1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:floor-correction}
 a_x(m)=\sum_{j=1}^m
 \left(
  \ceil{\frac{j(q+1)}r}-\frac{j(q+1)}r
  +\floor{\frac{j(q'+1)}r}-\frac{j(q'+1)}r
 \right).
\end{equation}
In particular \(a_x(m)>-m\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The cyclic form of quotient Riemann--Roch expresses the correction as
\[
 a_x(m)=\frac1r\sum_{\substack{\zeta^r=1\\\zeta\ne1}}
       \frac{\zeta^{-m(q+1)}-1}{(1-\zeta)(1-\zeta^q)}.
\]
This normalization has \(a_x(0)=0\). For an integer \(t\), set
\[
 S_r(t)=\frac1r\sum_{\substack{\zeta^r=1\\\zeta\ne1}}
                           \frac{\zeta^t}{1-\zeta}.
\]
If \(t_*\in\{1,\ldots,r\}\) represents \(t\bmod r\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sawtooth}
                         S_r(t)=\frac{2t_*-r-1}{2r}.
\end{equation}
Indeed \(S_r(0)=(r-1)/(2r)\), by pairing roots with their inverses.
Also \(S_r(t+1)-S_r(t)=1/r\) unless \(r\mid t\), when it is
\(-(r-1)/r\). This determines all values and proves
\eqref{eq:sawtooth}.

Using
\[
 \frac{1-\zeta^{q+1}}{(1-\zeta)(1-\zeta^q)}
                    =\frac1{1-\zeta^q}+\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}
\]
and replacing \(\zeta^q\) by a new root of unity in the first sum gives
\[
 a_x(m)-a_x(m-1)
        =S_r(-m(q'+1))+S_r(1-m(q+1)).
\]
Formula \eqref{eq:sawtooth} turns the right side into the summand
in \eqref{eq:floor-correction}. Summing from \(1\) to \(m\) proves
the formula. Its ceiling term minus its argument belongs to
\([0,1)\); its floor term minus its argument belongs to \((-1,0]\).
Each summand is therefore greater than \(-1\).
\end{proof}

For \(q=r-1\) the quotient is canonical and the formula gives zero.
The local period is
\[
                           d_x=\frac r{\gcd(r,q+1)}.
\]
These formulas permit all cyclic corrections to be calculated by
integer arithmetic followed by a single division by \(r\).

\subsection{A short exhaustive parametrization}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cyclic-parameters}
Every noncanonical cyclic quotient with \(\mld>1/11\) is obtained
from parameters \(e,r_1,q_1,t,n\) satisfying
\begin{align}
 &3\leq e\leq21,\quad (e-2)r_1<22,\quad
   (r_1,q_1)=(1,0)\ \text{or}\ 1\leq q_1<r_1,\ \gcd(r_1,q_1)=1,
       \label{eq:param-a}\\
 &\alpha=(e-1)r_1-q_1,\quad
   1\leq t\leq\min\{\alpha,10\},\quad
   n\geq\max\{r_1,t\},\quad\gcd(n,t)=1,\label{eq:param-b}\\
 &n=t\Longrightarrow n=t=1,\qquad
            (\alpha-11)n<r_1(11-t).\label{eq:param-c}
\end{align}
If \(q_1'\) is the inverse of \(q_1\bmod r_1\), with
\(q_1'=0\) for \(r_1=1\), and \(s=(q_1q_1'-1)/r_1\), the resulting
type is \(\frac1r(1,q)\), where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:param-rq}
             r=\alpha n+r_1t,\qquad
             q=((e-1)q_1'-s)n+q_1't.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a vertex where the smallest log discrepancy is attained.
It may be chosen with weight \(e\geq3\): a minimum at a vertex of
weight two propagates to its neighbors, and a noncanonical chain
has a vertex of weight at least three.
Read the two arms from this vertex outwards as
\(r_1/q_1\) and \(n/(n-t)\), interchanging them so that \(r_1\leq n\).
An empty arm is encoded by \((1,0)\).

Eliminating the arm equations gives the discrepancy at the central
vertex:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:central-cyclic}
                a_0=\frac{n+r_1}{\alpha n+r_1t}.
\end{equation}
The adjacent discrepancies are at most one, so \(ea_0\leq2\);
as \(a_0>1/11\), \(e\leq21\).
Furthermore
\[
 a_0<\frac{2}{(e-2)r_1},
\]
which proves the bound on \(r_1\).
The adjacent discrepancy on the second arm is
\(((n-t)a_0+1)/n\). Minimality at the center implies
\(ta_0\leq1\), equivalently \(t\leq\alpha\), and also \(t\leq10\).
The last inequality in \eqref{eq:param-c} is exactly
\(a_0>1/11\), after clearing denominators.
Finally, multiplying the continued-fraction matrices of the two
arms and the central vertex gives \eqref{eq:param-rq}.
The empty-arm convention gives \(s=-1\), so the same formula applies
without an exception.
\end{proof}

The displayed restrictions are necessary, which is what completeness
requires. The finite verification may include a germ more than once,
or parameters whose chosen center is not a minimum; it verifies
the claimed cost inequalities on this larger set.

For efficient enumeration write
\[
 n=(h+1)t+v,\qquad 0\leq v<t.
\]
The second arm is \(h\) entries equal to two followed by the
Hirzebruch--Jung chain of \((t+v)/v\), with an empty final chain if
\(v=0\). Thus its length and the sum of its weights minus two can
be computed without constructing a long intersection matrix.

\subsection{Noncyclic quotients}

A noncyclic quotient has a fork
\([e_0;(r_1,q_1),(r_2,q_2),(r_3,q_3)]\).
Here the three arms, read outwards from the central curve, have
continued fractions \(r_i/q_i\), and \(e_0\geq2\). Put
\[
             \sigma=\sum_{i=1}^3\frac1{r_i}-1,\qquad
             \eta=e_0-\sum_{i=1}^3\frac{q_i}{r_i}.
\]
The quotient classification gives \(\sigma,\eta>0\) and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fork-data}
 (r_1,r_2,r_3)\in\{(2,2,r),(2,3,3),(2,3,4),(2,3,5)\},
 \quad a_0=\frac{\sigma}{\eta},\quad g_x=\frac{4\eta}{\sigma^2}.
\end{equation}
The expression for \(a_0\) can also be seen directly: the first
discrepancy on arm \(i\) is \((q_i a_0+1)/r_i\), and the central
equation is \(e_0a_0-\sum_i(q_i a_0+1)/r_i=-1\).
For the group-order formula see \cite[Lemma 2.10]{LL26stable}.
The determinant of \(A_x\), in contrast, is \(r_1r_2r_3\eta\).

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fork-extension}
Let \(d\geq2\), \(1\leq t<d\), \(\gcd(d,t)=1\), and \(u\geq1\).
For the germs
\[
 F_u=[2;(2,1),(2,1),(ud+t,(u-1)d+t)]
\]
one has
\[
 \ell(F_u)=\ell(F_1)+u-1,\quad
 \gamma(F_u)=\gamma(F_1)+u-1,\quad
 a_{F_u}(m)=a_{F_1}(m)\quad(m\geq0).
\]
Moreover \(g(F_u)=4d(ud+t)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The third arm of \(F_u\) is obtained by inserting \(u-1\)
curves of weight two next to the center of \(F_1\).
The central discrepancy is \(1/d\), and the discrepancy at the
first vertex of the third arm of \(F_1\) is also \(1/d\).
The discrepancy equations show that the inserted vertices all have
discrepancy \(1/d\), with the other discrepancies unchanged.
They have \(k_i=0\), so \(k^tb\) does not change. This proves
the claims about \(\ell\) and \(\gamma\).

In \eqref{eq:local-correction}, let
\(\epsilon=\{(m+1)(1-1/d)\}\). At the center, the inserted vertices,
and the old first vertex, the fractional part is \(\epsilon\).
The insertions add \(2(u-1)\epsilon^2\) to the diagonal terms of
\(f^tAf\). They replace one edge by \(u\) edges and hence subtract
the same amount from the off-diagonal terms. The linear term is
unchanged because all inserted \(k_i\) vanish. Thus the corrections
are unchanged. Finally \(\sigma=1/(ud+t)\) and
\(\eta=d/(ud+t)\), giving the group order in \eqref{eq:fork-data}.
\end{proof}

This proof is included to make explicit the control of an infinite
noncyclic family; compare \cite[Lemma 9.5]{LL26stable}.

\section{Positive local costs and complete catalogues}\label{sec:cost}

\subsection{Two choices of weights}

We use two costs, indexed by \(h\in\{10,11\}\). Set \(S=10000\) and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost-definition}
 \cost_x^{(h)}
   =\frac1S\left(A_h\mu_x+\sum_{m=1}^h W_m^{(h)}a_x(m)\right).
\end{equation}
The integers \(A_h\) and \(W_m^{(h)}\) are as follows:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|rr}
\toprule
 & \(h=11\)&\(h=10\)\\
\midrule
\(A_h\)&1332160&1328286\\
\(W_1^{(h)}\)&2626097&2646679\\
\(W_2^{(h)}\)&2448046&2502711\\
\(W_3^{(h)}\)&2149838&2225722\\
\(W_4^{(h)}\)&1750133&1676569\\
\(W_5^{(h)}\)&1451008&1489733\\
\(W_6^{(h)}\)&1087041&1041192\\
\(W_7^{(h)}\)&736627&678175\\
\(W_8^{(h)}\)&487293&458179\\
\(W_9^{(h)}\)&306241&302742\\
\(W_{10}^{(h)}\)&112769&39074\\
\(W_{11}^{(h)}\)&42854&0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the second column the final zero is only a convenient extension
of the notation.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost-budgets}
 U_h=\frac{12A_h-\sum_mW_m^{(h)}}S,\qquad
 U_{11}=\frac{2787973}{10000},\qquad
 U_{10}=\frac{179916}{625}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:global-cost}
Suppose \(X\) is exceptional and \(P_1=\cdots=P_h=0\).
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:global-cost}
 \sum_x\cost_x^{(h)}
 \leq U_h-
 \left(\frac{4A_h}{3S}+
       \frac1S\sum_{m=1}^hW_m^{(h)}Q_m\right)V
 <U_h.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Each vanishing in \eqref{eq:RR} gives
\(\sum_xa_x(m)=-1-Q_mV\). Multiply these equations by
\(W_m^{(h)}/S\), multiply \eqref{eq:budget-mu} by \(A_h/S\),
and add. All weights are nonnegative and \(V>0\).
\end{proof}

The next proposition is the local input. Its proof separates the
infinite families from the finite arithmetic.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:cost-positivity}
Let \(x\) be a singular quotient surface germ with \(\mld(x)>1/11\).
For \(h=10,11\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost-positive}
                         \cost_x^{(h)}>0.
\end{equation}
For a cyclic germ of order \(r\geq30\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost-order}
                   \cost_x^{(h)}\geq\frac{99}{100}(r-1).
\end{equation}
Among the \(63\) noncyclic base configurations described below, the
minimum costs are respectively
\[
 \min\cost^{(11)}=\frac{8254537}{120000},\qquad
 \min\cost^{(10)}=\frac{10793489}{160000}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\subsection{The entire cyclic tail}

Put \(\kappa=99/100\) and
\[
 D_h=\frac{37}2A_h+\sum_{m=1}^h mW_m^{(h)}.
\]
Lemmas~\ref{lem:cyclic-identities} and
\ref{lem:cyclic-correction} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tail-basic}
       S\cost_x^{(h)}>A_h\ell-D_h,\qquad
       r-1<121\ell-100.
\end{equation}
Both \(A_h-121\kappa S\) are positive. Thus
\[
 \ell\geq L_h:=
 \ceil{\frac{D_h-100\kappa S}{A_h-121\kappa S}}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 A_h\ell-D_h\geq\kappa S(121\ell-100).
\]
Direct integer arithmetic gives
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrr}
\toprule
\(h\)&\(D_h\)&\(L_h\)&temporary order cutoff \(R_h=121L_h\)\\
\midrule
11&72804467&535&64735\\
10&70832726&536&64856\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For all chains of length at least \(L_h\), \eqref{eq:tail-basic}
therefore proves \eqref{eq:cost-order}, and in particular positivity.
If \(\ell<L_h\), then \(r<121\ell-99<R_h\).
It remains to verify a finite set of chains with \(r\leq R_h\).
The cutoff is deliberately slightly larger than necessary.

\subsection{The finite cyclic calculation}

Apply the parametrization in Lemma~\ref{lem:cyclic-parameters}.
For fixed \(e,r_1,q_1,t\), the remaining variable \(n\) belongs to
the interval
\begin{equation}\label{eq:n-range}
 \max\{r_1,t\}\leq n\leq
        \floor{\frac{R_h-r_1t}{\alpha}}.
\end{equation}
If \(\alpha>11\), also impose
\[
 n\leq\floor{\frac{r_1(11-t)-1}{\alpha-11}}.
\]
When \(\alpha\leq11\) the latter restriction is automatic:
its left side in \eqref{eq:param-c} is nonpositive, whereas
\(r_1(11-t)>0\).
Retain only \(\gcd(n,t)=1\), and apply the empty-arm convention.

With \(r,q,\ell,B\) computed from the parameters,
\eqref{eq:mu-cyclic} and \eqref{eq:floor-correction} give
\begin{align}
 rS\cost_x^{(h)}
 &=A_h\bigl(r(\ell-B+3)-q-q'-3\bigr)\notag\\
 &\quad+\sum_{m=1}^hW_m^{(h)}
 \left[
 r\sum_{j=1}^m
 \left(\ceil{\frac{j(q+1)}r}+
       \floor{\frac{j(q'+1)}r}\right)
       -Q_m(q+q'+2)\right].\label{eq:integer-cost}
\end{align}
The right side is an integer.
The finite calculation tests that it is positive and, for \(r\geq30\),
that it is at least \(99Sr(r-1)/100\).
Equivalently, multiply the latter comparison by \(100\).
The calculation tests \(1,694,986\) parameter tuples for \(h=11\)
and \(1,698,152\) tuples for \(h=10\); all inequalities hold.
The loop and arithmetic used to verify this statement are specified
in Appendix~\ref{app:verification}, and are included in the
attached executable source.

Canonical cyclic germs were omitted from the minimum-vertex
parametrization. For \(\frac1r(1,r-1)\) the correction is zero and
\[
               \cost_x^{(h)}=\frac{A_h}{S}\left(r-\frac1r\right).
\]
Since \(A_h/S>\kappa\), both required inequalities hold.
This completes the cyclic proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:cost-positivity}.

\subsection{A finite set of noncyclic bases}

We now give all necessary parameter bounds for the noncyclic case.
This also records why arbitrarily long dihedral arms cannot be
missed by the finite calculation.

For arm orders \((2,2,r)\), if \(e_0\geq3\), put
\[
                         d=(e_0-1)r-q.
\]
The central discrepancy is \(1/d\), so \(d\leq10\).
As \(q\leq r-1\), this implies
\[
                    (e_0-2)r+1\leq10,\qquad e_0\leq6.
\]
Thus there are finitely many coprime choices of \(r,q\).
After the full discrepancy test \(a_i>1/11\), they give \(22\)
bases.

If \(e_0=2\), put \(d=r-q\).
The case \(d=1\) is the canonical \(D\)-series. It has \(\ell\geq4\),
\(g_x\geq8\), and zero correction, hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:canonical-D}
            \cost_x^{(h)}\geq\frac{A_h}{S}\frac{39}{8}>U_h.
\end{equation}
For \(2\leq d\leq10\), write uniquely
\[
 r=ud+t,\quad q=(u-1)d+t,\quad u\geq1,\quad
 1\leq t<d,\quad \gcd(d,t)=1.
\]
There are \(31\) bases with \(u=1\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:fork-extension}, the correction is independent of \(u\),
whereas \(\gamma\) increases by \(u-1\) and \(g_x\) increases.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fork-growth}
                  \cost_{F_u}^{(h)}
       \geq\cost_{F_1}^{(h)}+\frac{A_h}{S}(u-1).
\end{equation}

For the three \(E\)-types, the inequality \(q_i\leq r_i-1\)
gives \(\sum_iq_i/r_i\leq2-\sigma\).
Since \(a_0=\sigma/\eta>1/11\),
\[
                           e_0<2+10\sigma.
\]
Thus \(e_0\in\{2,3\}\) for \((2,3,3)\), and \(e_0=2\) for
\((2,3,4)\) and \((2,3,5)\).
Enumerate all coprime arm residues, identify permutations of equal
arms, and impose \(a_i>1/11\). There are \(10\) bases.

In total these are \(22+31+10=63\) bases.
For each base the verifier forms \(A_x\), solves \(A_xb_x=k_x\)
over \(\Q\), and computes
\eqref{eq:local-correction} over one local period.
The minimum costs are those in
Proposition~\ref{prop:cost-positivity}, and are positive.
Together with \eqref{eq:canonical-D} and \eqref{eq:fork-growth},
this proves positivity for every noncyclic germ.
In addition, for both choices of \(h\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fork-cutoff}
            \min_{\rm bases}\cost^{(h)}+\frac{2A_h}{S}>U_h.
\end{equation}
Thus a germ satisfying the global cost budget can have only
\(u=1\) or \(u=2\).

\subsection{The complete catalogues}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:catalogues}
Suppose \(X\) is exceptional, \(\mld(X)>1/11\), and
\(P_1=\cdots=P_h=0\). Every singularity of \(X\) belongs to the
finite catalogue \(\ccat_h\) specified by the preceding parameter
ranges and the filter \(\cost_x^{(h)}<U_h\).
The catalogues have the following sizes:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrr}
\toprule
\(h\)&maximum cyclic order&cyclic types&noncyclic types&total\\
\midrule
11&282&2063&51&2114\\
10&291&2067&54&2121\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Cyclic types are normalized by \(q\leq q'\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Every local cost is positive, while their sum is strictly less than
\(U_h\). Hence each local cost is strictly less than \(U_h\).
For \(r\geq30\), \eqref{eq:cost-order} gives
\(\kappa(r-1)<U_h\), so \(r\leq\ceil{U_h/\kappa}\).
These bounds are \(282\) and \(291\).
The smaller orders are already included.
The noncyclic restrictions follow from
\eqref{eq:canonical-D} and \eqref{eq:fork-cutoff}.

For each cyclic pair up to the resulting order bound, calculate the
chain, its discrepancies and its cost. Retain it if
\(\gcd(r,q)=1\), \(q\leq q'\), all discrepancies are \(>1/11\),
and its cost is \(<U_h\).
Apply the same last two tests to the fork list, including the allowed
\(u=2\) extensions. Exact enumeration gives the displayed counts.
\end{proof}

The two catalogues have different hypotheses. The \(2114\)-type
catalogue requires eleven initial vanishings, and cannot be used
when \(b=11\). The \(2121\)-type catalogue was constructed precisely
for the latter case.

\section{Nonvanishing in the small-volume range}\label{sec:nonvanishing}

\subsection{The linear constraints}

For the moment use \(\ccat_{11}\), and let \(n_j\) denote the
multiplicity of type \(j\).
It is useful to allow arbitrary \(n_j\in\mathbb R_{\geq0}\).
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u-definition}
 c_j=a_j(1),\quad u_j(m)=a_j(m)-Q_mc_j,\quad
 k_j=\gamma_j-c_j,\quad \nu_j=\mu_j-\tfrac43c_j.
\end{equation}
The vanishing \(P_1=0\) eliminates \(V\) from
\eqref{eq:RR}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-RR}
       V=-1-\sum_jc_jn_j,\qquad
       P_m=1-Q_m+\sum_ju_j(m)n_j.
\end{equation}
The following four inequalities are necessary:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:four-global}
 \sum_jc_jn_j\leq-1,\quad
 \sum_jk_jn_j\leq10,\quad
 \sum_j\nu_jn_j\leq\frac{40}{3},\quad
 \sum_j\cost_j^{(11)}n_j\leq U_{11}.
\end{equation}
The first and last weaken strict inequalities.
For the second, \eqref{eq:noether} gives
\(\rho(X)=11-\sum_jk_jn_j\geq1\).
The third is \eqref{eq:budget-mu} after eliminating \(V\).

If \(P_1=\cdots=P_{d-1}=0\), it is equivalent to impose, in addition
to \(P_1=0\),
\begin{align}
 \sum_ju_j(2)n_j&=2,\notag\\
 \sum_j\bigl(u_j(m+1)-2u_j(m)+u_j(m-1)\bigr)n_j&=1
                           \quad(2\leq m\leq d-2).
                         \label{eq:zero-second-diff}
\end{align}
This follows because \(Q_{m+1}-2Q_m+Q_{m-1}=1\).
The equalities telescope, so no vanishing has been lost.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:multiplication}
If \(P_d>0\), then \(P_{n+d}\geq P_n\) for every \(n\geq0\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Represent a nonzero section of \(\OO_X(-dK_X)\) by a rational
function \(s\) with
\(\divi(s)-dK_X\geq0\).
Multiplication by \(s\) maps the rational functions representing
sections in degree \(n\) into those in degree \(n+d\).
It is injective because \(X\) is integral.
This argument uses divisorial inequalities, so it applies to
reflexive sheaves without assuming that \(dK_X\) is Cartier.
\end{proof}

If \(b=d\), use \(P_d\geq1\), \(P_n\geq0\), and the lemma.
Their normalized forms are
\begin{align}
 -\frac1{Q_d}\sum_ju_j(d)n_j&\leq-1,\label{eq:first-row}\\
 -\frac1{Q_n}\sum_ju_j(n)n_j&\leq\frac{1-Q_n}{Q_n},
                                      \label{eq:nonneg-row}\\
 -\frac{\sum_j(u_j(n+d)-u_j(n))n_j}{Q_{n+d}-Q_n}&\leq-1.
                                      \label{eq:shift-row}
\end{align}
For the finite systems below it is enough to use total degree at most
\(220\).

\subsection{Exact contradiction certificates}

We state the elementary certificate principle to fix the signs.
Suppose \(n\geq0\), \(En=e\), and \(An\leq b_0\).
If a signed vector \(y\) and a nonnegative vector \(\lambda\) satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:farkas}
       y^tE+\lambda^tA\geq0
       \quad\hbox{coefficientwise},\qquad
       y^te+\lambda^tb_0<0,
\end{equation}
then the system is impossible: the first expression applied to
\(n\) is nonnegative and is bounded above by the second.
All finite contradictions in this section use this principle.
The multipliers are explicit rational numbers in the attachments.
The checks of \eqref{eq:farkas} are exact.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:29}
Let \(X\) be exceptional with \(\mld(X)>1/11\). Then \(b(X)\leq29\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If \(b\leq11\), there is nothing to prove. Otherwise
Corollary~\ref{cor:catalogues} applies with \(h=11\).

First suppose \(P_1=\cdots=P_{66}=0\).
Use the \(65\) equations consisting of the first equality of
\eqref{eq:zero-second-diff} and the second differences for
\(2\leq m\leq65\).
The certificate in \texttt{efv\_nonvanishing\_dual.json} combines
these with \eqref{eq:four-global}.
Its coefficient on the last cost inequality is \(3/1012\).
After combination, the smallest coefficient of any \(n_j\) is
\[
                  \frac{4991}{90000000000000000}>0,
\]
whereas the right side is
\[
                 -\frac{118789586081}{10000000000000000}<0.
\]
Thus \(b\leq66\).

Next assume \(b=d\) for \(35\leq d\leq66\), or \(d=33\).
Use \eqref{eq:four-global},
\eqref{eq:zero-second-diff}, and
\eqref{eq:first-row}--\eqref{eq:shift-row} through degree \(220\).
The \(33\) certificates \texttt{efv\_first\_d\_dual.json},
where \(d=33,35,\ldots,66\),
verify \eqref{eq:farkas} in each case.
Hence only \(d\leq32\) or \(d=34\) remain.

For \(d=30,31,32,34\), use also \(P_n\in\Z_{\geq0}\).
If the current constraints together with \(P_n\geq1\) admit a
certificate of the form \eqref{eq:farkas}, then \(P_n=0\).
Add that equality and continue. The successive forced zeros are:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{c|l|r}
\toprule
\(d\)&new zero degrees, in order&certificates\\
\midrule
30&31, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 37&8\\
31&32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 37, 39&8\\
32&33, 34, 35, 36, 37, 38, 40&8\\
34&35, 36, 37, 38, 40, 41, 42, 43, 44&10\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For each row, one certificate proves each new zero and a final
certificate contradicts the enlarged system.
These are the files \texttt{efv\_first\_d\_integrality\_duals.json}
for \(d=30,31,32,34\).
The checker permits a zero equality only after its corresponding
contradiction has been verified. Thus the argument is induction on
the displayed lists, rather than an assertion of integer-programming
infeasibility.
This excludes all \(d\geq30\).
\end{proof}

The proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:29} uses no volume ceiling.
We need only its stated exceptional, high-discrepancy version;
no complement reduction for general del Pezzo surfaces is used here.

\subsection{Focused nonvanishing}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:focused}
Let \(X\) be an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface with
\(V\leq1/31801\). Then
\[
                         \mld(X)>1/11,\qquad b(X)\leq11.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Liu--Shokurov classify the exceptional del Pezzo surfaces with
\(\mld\leq1/11\) into \(25\) isomorphism classes
\cite[Theorems 1.9 and 8.8, Table 8]{LS23}.
The minimum of their listed volumes is \(1/21385\), attained
by row \(11\); see also their Remark 8.12.
It is larger than \(1/31801\), so \(\mld(X)>1/11\).

Proposition~\ref{prop:29} gives \(b\leq29\).
Suppose \(b=d\in\{12,\ldots,29\}\).
The catalogue \(\ccat_{11}\) applies.
Add to the system in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:29} the row
\begin{equation}\label{eq:volume-ceiling}
                    -\sum_jc_jn_j\leq1+\frac1{31801},
\end{equation}
which is precisely the assumed volume ceiling in
\eqref{eq:linear-RR}.
For each \(d\), the file \texttt{efv\_small\_volume\_duals.json}
contains a rational certificate satisfying \eqref{eq:farkas}.
The numbers of labeled rows are
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrrrrr}
\toprule
\(d\)&12&13&14&15&16&17&18&19&20\\
\midrule
rows&47&45&41&39&57&65&51&67&63\\
\midrule
\(d\)&21&22&23&24&25&26&27&28&29\\
\midrule
rows&19&53&64&68&38&54&65&41&61\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
No further integrality step is required in these \(18\) cases.
For example, in degree \(13\) the minimum combined coefficient is
\[
 \frac{955996659047993847}
 {15478863208076279375000000000000000}>0,
\]
and the combined right side is
\[
 -\frac{8987219403261730627}{159005000000000000000000}<0.
\]
The checker verifies the corresponding signs for all local types
and every \(d\) in the table. Thus none of these first degrees occurs.
\end{proof}

The argument is not circular. The discrepancy threshold comes from
the volumes in a previously published classification, the finite
catalogue comes from eleven vanishings and the orbifold inequality,
and the preliminary bound \(29\) was obtained without the new
volume bound. The volume ceiling appears only in
\eqref{eq:volume-ceiling}.

\section{First anticanonical members and support models}\label{sec:support}

The numerical argument has reduced the first degree to at most eleven.
We now use a first member to produce a stable pair with a reduced
boundary. We prove the construction with the anticanonical sign
throughout.

\subsection{The arithmetic genus of a first member}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:first-genus}
Let \(X\) be a klt del Pezzo surface and
\(M\in|-mK_X|\), \(m\geq1\).
Give \(M\) the divisorial subscheme structure defined by the ideal
\(\OO_X(-M)\subset\OO_X\).
Then \(M\) is Cohen--Macaulay and
\[
 h^0(M,\OO_M)=1,\qquad
 h^1(M,\OO_M)=p_a(M)=P_{m-1}.
\]
In particular, if \(m=b(X)>1\), the reduced support of \(M\) is
connected and each of its irreducible components has normalization
\(\PP^1\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The surface \(X\) is Cohen--Macaulay. A rank-one reflexive sheaf on
a normal surface has depth two at every closed point.
Thus \(F=\OO_X(-M)\) is maximal Cohen--Macaulay.
The depth lemma applied to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-exact}
                  0\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow\OO_X
                       \longrightarrow\OO_M\longrightarrow0
\end{equation}
shows that \(M\) is a Cohen--Macaulay curve.

For a maximal Cohen--Macaulay sheaf and a dualizing sheaf,
\(\mathcal Ext^q(F,\omega_X)=0\) for \(q>0\).
Here
\[
 \mathcal Hom(F,\omega_X)
       =\OO_X(K_X+M)
       \simeq\OO_X(-(m-1)K_X).
\]
Serre duality therefore gives
\[
 H^1(X,F)^*\simeq H^1(X,\OO_X(-(m-1)K_X))=0
\]
by \eqref{eq:weil-kv}, and
\[
 H^2(X,F)^*\simeq H^0(X,\OO_X(-(m-1)K_X)).
\]
Also \(H^1(X,\OO_X)=H^2(X,\OO_X)=0\).
Since \(M\ne0\) is effective, \(H^0(X,F)=0\).
The cohomology sequence of \eqref{eq:first-exact} gives
the two asserted dimensions.

Now \(m=b>1\) implies \(P_{b-1}=0\).
Let \(D=M_{\rm red}\).
The support is connected because \(H^0(\OO_M)=\C\).
The kernel of \(\OO_M\to\OO_D\) is supported in dimension at most
one, so \(H^1(\OO_D)=0\).
For the normalizations \(D_i^\nu\) of the irreducible components,
the normalization sequence gives
\[
 0=p_a(D)=\sum_i g(D_i^\nu)+\sum_{p\in D}\delta_p-\#\{D_i\}+1.
\]
Connectedness implies \(\sum_p\delta_p\geq\#\{D_i\}-1\).
Every genus is nonnegative, so each is zero.
\end{proof}

This genus calculation does not assume that \(M\) is Cartier,
reduced, or irreducible. It will also account for the rationality
of a boundary component that survives a birational construction.

\subsection{The reduced-support construction}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:support}
Let \(X\) be exceptional and let \(M\in|-mK_X|\).
Choose a log resolution \(f:W\to X\), and let \(B_W\) be the
reduced union of the strict support of \(M\) and all \(f\)-exceptional
curves. The pair \((W,B_W)\) has a log canonical model
\(g:W\to T\).
Writing \(C=g_*B_W\), the pair \((T,C)\) is lc,
\(C\ne0\) is connected, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:support-volume}
                         0<(K_T+C)^2\leq(m-1)^2V.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(X\) is exceptional, \((X,M/m)\) is klt. Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gamma}
                       K_W+\Gamma=f^*(K_X+M/m)\sim_{\Q}0.
\end{equation}
The divisor \(\Gamma\) is supported on \(B_W\), and every coefficient
of \(\Gamma\) is strictly less than one. It need not be effective.
The divisor \(B_W-\Gamma\), however, is effective and has strictly
positive coefficient on every component of \(B_W\).
The effective divisor \(f^*M\) is supported on \(B_W\).
Because there are only finitely many components, there is an
\(\epsilon>0\) with
\[
                       B_W-\Gamma\geq\epsilon f^*M.
\]
The right side is big, since \(M\sim_{\Q}-mK_X\).
Consequently \(K_W+B_W\sim_{\Q}B_W-\Gamma\) is big.
The surface log minimal model program and abundance give its
log canonical model. In dimension two the contractions compose
to a morphism \(g:W\to T\); \(K_T+C\) is ample.

To compare volumes, put \(D=\Supp M\) and
\[
                      F=\sum_{E\subset\Exc(f)}a(E,X,0)E\geq0.
\]
Expanding the canonical divisor formula gives
\[
 K_W+B_W=f^*K_X+F+f_*^{-1}D
              \leq f^*(K_X+M)+F.
\]
Adding an effective exceptional divisor to the pullback of a big
divisor does not change its volume. Indeed, for sufficiently
divisible integral multiples, a rational function with allowed
poles only along an exceptional divisor extends downstairs across
codimension two by normality. The spaces of sections of the two
divisors are therefore the same.
Since \(K_X+M\sim_{\Q}(m-1)(-K_X)\), monotonicity of volume gives
\eqref{eq:support-volume}.

Write \(C=\sum C_i\) and
\(g_*\Gamma=\sum\gamma_iC_i\), with \(\gamma_i<1\).
Pushing \eqref{eq:Gamma} down gives
\[
                    K_T+C\sim_{\Q}\sum_i(1-\gamma_i)C_i.
\]
If \(C=0\), the ample divisor on the left would be
\(\Q\)-linearly trivial, a contradiction.
The divisor on the right is effective and ample, so its support is
connected.
For completeness, if an effective ample \(\Q\)-Cartier divisor on
a normal projective surface had two disjoint nonzero parts \(A,B\),
these parts would themselves be \(\Q\)-Cartier locally on their
disjoint supports. One would have
\(A\cdot B=0\), \(A^2=(A+B)\cdot A>0\), and \(B^2>0\).
Pulling back to a resolution contradicts the Hodge index theorem.
Thus \(C\) is connected.
\end{proof}

The sign in \eqref{eq:support-volume} is important.
The comparison uses \(K_X+M\), whose numerical class is
\((m-1)(-K_X)\), and an effective exceptional divisor.
It does not follow by replacing \(K_X\) with \(-K_X\) in a
general-type volume comparison.

\subsection{Small volume gives a rational plt boundary}

We use the following precise external input.
If \((T,C)\) is a projective lc surface pair, \(C\ne0\) is reduced,
\(K_T+C\) is ample, and \(C\) contains a zero-dimensional non-klt
center, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Liu-Shao}
                         (K_T+C)^2\geq\frac1{42}
\end{equation}
by \cite[Theorem 1.3]{LS26}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:small-support}
In Proposition~\ref{prop:support}, suppose \(m=b(X)>1\) and
\((m-1)^2V<1/42\). Then \(C\) is an ample smooth rational curve,
\((T,C)\) is globally plt, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:support-p}
               K_T+pC\sim_{\Q}0,\qquad 0<p<1,\qquad mp\in\Z.
\end{equation}
Moreover \((T,pC)\) and \((X,M/m)\) are crepant birational klt pairs.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:Liu-Shao}, \(C\) contains no zero-dimensional non-klt
center of \((T,C)\).
The local classification of lc surface pairs then implies that
\((T,C)\) is plt in a neighborhood of \(C\): any failure of plt
along a reduced boundary produces a zero-dimensional lc center.
The components of \(C\) are therefore smooth and pairwise disjoint.
Connectedness from Proposition~\ref{prop:support} makes \(C\) a single
smooth curve.

Let \(E\) be the unique component of \(B_W\) mapping onto \(C\).
Every component of the strict support of \(M\) is rational by
Proposition~\ref{prop:first-genus}. The exceptional curves of \(f\)
are rational as well: the minimal resolution of a quotient singularity
has rational exceptional curves, and further blowups preserve this
property. Thus \(E\), and hence \(C\), is rational.

The divisor \(g_*\Gamma\) is now \(pC\), for a rational \(p<1\).
Consequently
\[
              K_T+C\sim_{\Q}(1-p)C.
\]
This shows that \(C\) is ample. Since \(T\) is plt near \(C\),
it is \(\Q\)-factorial there; away from \(C\), the divisor \(C\) is
zero. Hence \(C\) is globally \(\Q\)-Cartier, as are
\(K_T\) and \(K_T+pC\).

Plt adjunction gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adjunction-C}
 \lambda:=(K_T+C)\cdot C
          =-2+\sum_i(1-1/r_i)>0,
\end{equation}
where the marked points are the singular points of \(T\) on \(C\),
with local cyclic indices \(r_i\geq2\).
For a projective line with standard coefficients, every positive
degree in \eqref{eq:adjunction-C} is at least \(1/42\);
an elementary verification is given in
Lemma~\ref{lem:orbifold-line} below.
It follows that
\[
 0<1-p=\frac{(K_T+C)^2}{\lambda}<1.
\]
Thus \(0<p<1\).

Choose a rational function \(h\) with
\(M=-mK_X+\divi_X(h)\).
Multiplying \eqref{eq:Gamma} by \(m\) gives the exact divisor identity
\[
                       mK_W+m\Gamma=\divi_W(h).
\]
Pushing down to \(T\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mp-integral}
                        mK_T+mpC=\divi_T(h).
\end{equation}
Both \(mK_T\) and \(\divi_T(h)\) are integral Weil divisors.
Since \(C\) is prime, \(mp\) is an integer.
This argument applies also when \(E\) was exceptional over \(X\).

Finally,
\[
             (K_W+\Gamma)-g^*(K_T+pC)
\]
is \(g\)-exceptional and numerically trivial.
The negative definiteness of the exceptional intersection matrix
forces it to be zero.
Thus the two pairs are crepant birational.
Since \((X,M/m)\) is klt, so is \((T,pC)\).
In particular \(T\) is klt away from \(C\); together with the
already established plt property near \(C\), this proves that
\((T,C)\) is globally plt.
\end{proof}

The rationality argument in this proof uses the fact that \(M\)
is a \emph{first} member. It avoids any additional classification of
high boundary coefficients or equality pairs.

\section{The first degree is eleven}\label{sec:eleven}

\subsection{Adjunction on an orbifold projective line}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:orbifold-line}
Let \(r_1,\ldots,r_s\geq2\) be integers and suppose
\[
                    \lambda=-2+\sum_{i=1}^s(1-1/r_i)>0.
\]
Then \(\lambda\geq1/42\).
If \(0<\lambda<1/24\), then \(s=3\), the unordered signature is
\((2,3,7)\), and \(\lambda=1/42\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The cases \(s\leq2\) give nonpositive degree.
If \(s\geq5\), then \(\lambda\geq s/2-2\geq1/2\).
If \(s=4\), all indices two give zero, and any increase gives at
least \(1/6\).

For \(s=3\), order the indices so that
\(r_1\leq r_2\leq r_3\).
If \(r_1\geq4\), the degree is at least \(1/4\).
If \(r_1=3\), the triple \((3,3,3)\) has degree zero and the
smallest positive degree is \(1/12\), from \((3,3,4)\).
Thus a degree below \(1/24\) requires \(r_1=2\).
For \(r_2\geq5\), the degree is at least \(1/10\).
For \(r_2=4\), the smallest positive degree is \(1/20\),
from \((2,4,5)\).
For \(r_2=2\), the degree is negative.
The remaining possibility is \((2,3,r_3)\), where positivity means
\(r_3\geq7\). The values at \(r_3=7\) and \(8\) are respectively
\(1/42\) and \(1/24\), and the degree increases with \(r_3\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fractional self-intersection}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:intersection-congruence}
Let \((T,C)\) be a plt surface pair with \(C\) a smooth prime curve.
Suppose the singular points of \(T\) on \(C\) have indices
\(r_1,\ldots,r_s\).
Then there exist integers \(Q_i\), with
\(1\leq Q_i<r_i\) and \(\gcd(Q_i,r_i)=1\), such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-intersection}
                         C^2\equiv\sum_i\frac{Q_i}{r_i}\pmod{\Z}.
\end{equation}
In particular, for signature \((2,3,7)\), \(42C^2\) is an odd integer.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A plt surface germ along its reduced boundary is a cyclic quotient
of a smooth surface with a coordinate-axis boundary.
On the minimal resolution, the strict transform \(\widetilde C\)
meets one end of the Hirzebruch--Jung chain transversely.
If \(v\) is the corresponding unit vector and \(A\) the positive
intersection matrix, the exceptional coefficients of the numerical
pullback of \(C\) are \(A^{-1}v\).
Thus the local correction to \(\widetilde C^2\) is
\(v^tA^{-1}v\).
The end diagonal entries of \(A^{-1}\) are \(q/r\) and \(q'/r\).
In either orientation this is \(Q/r\) with \(Q\) prime to \(r\).
The integral number \(\widetilde C^2\) disappears modulo \(\Z\),
which proves \eqref{eq:local-intersection}.

For indices \(2,3,7\), multiplication by \(42\) gives
\[
       42C^2\equiv21+14Q_3+6Q_7\pmod{42},
\]
whose right side is odd.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:degree-eleven}
If \(X\) is exceptional and \(V\leq1/31801\), then \(b(X)=11\).
For every \(M\in|-11K_X|\), its support model satisfies
\[
                  p=\frac{10}{11},\qquad
                  (K_T+C)^2=\frac1{462},\qquad
                  C^2=\frac{11}{42}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:focused} gives \(m=b\leq11\), while
exceptionality gives \(m\geq2\).
Since
\[
             (m-1)^2V\leq\frac{100}{31801}<\frac1{42},
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-support} applies.
Set \(k=m(1-p)\). It is a positive integer by
\eqref{eq:support-p}. Equations
\eqref{eq:support-volume} and \eqref{eq:adjunction-C} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adjunction-squeeze}
       \frac{k}{42m}\leq(1-p)\lambda=(K_T+C)^2
                      \leq\frac{(m-1)^2}{31801}.
\end{equation}
If \(m\leq9\), this would imply
\[
                  31801\leq42m(m-1)^2\leq24192,
\]
which is impossible.
For \(m=10\) or \(11\), \eqref{eq:adjunction-squeeze} gives
\[
                     k\leq\frac{46200}{31801}<2,
\]
so \(k=1\) and \(1-p=1/m\).
Moreover
\[
             0<\lambda=m(K_T+C)^2
                     \leq\frac{1100}{31801}<\frac1{24}.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:orbifold-line}, the signature is \((2,3,7)\)
and \(\lambda=1/42\).
Therefore \(C^2=m\lambda=m/42\).

Lemma~\ref{lem:intersection-congruence} makes \(m=42C^2\) odd,
excluding \(m=10\).
Hence \(m=11\), \(p=10/11\), and
\((K_T+C)^2=(1-p)\lambda=1/462\).
\end{proof}

Only the numerical data of the support model were used.
Identifying the pair up to isomorphism is unnecessary for the
sharp volume inequality.

\section{The sharp rational inequality}\label{sec:sharp}

\subsection{A certificate on the ten-vanishing catalogue}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:sharp-degree}
Let \(X\) be an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface with
\(\mld(X)>1/11\) and \(b(X)=11\).
Then \(V\geq1/31801\).
If equality holds, \(P_{11}=1\).
\end{theorem}

We prove this theorem without imposing any volume upper bound.
Since \(P_1=\cdots=P_{10}=0\),
Corollary~\ref{cor:catalogues} gives the catalogue \(\ccat_{10}\)
with \(2121\) types.
Let \(n_j\geq0\) be the local multiplicities, and use
\(c_j=a_j(1)\), \(u_j(m)=a_j(m)-Q_mc_j\) as before.
Then \eqref{eq:linear-RR} holds, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-equalities}
                 \sum_ju_j(m)n_j=Q_m-1\qquad(2\leq m\leq10).
\end{equation}

For the sharp certificate it is convenient to use unscaled rows
with the inequality direction \(\geq\).
Write \(R_i n\geq\beta_i\) for the following necessary conditions:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
\toprule
row&coefficient \(R_{ij}\)&right side \(\beta_i\)\\
\midrule
\(F\)&\(u_j(11)\)&\(Q_{11}=66\)\\
\(N_m\)&\(u_j(m)\)&\(Q_m-1\)\\
\(S_n\)&\(u_j(n+11)-u_j(n)\)&\(Q_{n+11}-Q_n\)\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The row \(F\) is \(P_{11}\geq1\), the rows \(N_m\) are
\(P_m\geq0\), and the rows \(S_n\) are multiplication inequalities
from Lemma~\ref{lem:multiplication}.

There are positive rational numbers \(t_i\) on \(66\) such rows,
and signed rational numbers \(y_2,\ldots,y_{10}\), for which
\begin{align}
 r_j:=-c_j-\sum_i t_iR_{ij}
                  -\sum_{m=2}^{10}y_mu_j(m)&\geq0
                                \quad(j\in\ccat_{10}),\label{eq:sharp-residual}\\
 \sum_i t_i\beta_i+\sum_{m=2}^{10}y_m(Q_m-1)&=
                                   \frac{31802}{31801}.\label{eq:sharp-constant}
\end{align}
Appendix~\ref{app:sharp-table} prints all coefficients over a common
denominator. The selected nonnegativity degrees are
\[
                            m=13,23,40,61,64.
\]
The other \(60\) rows are multiplication rows, whose largest total
degree is \(211\). The remaining row is \(F\).
All local residuals in \eqref{eq:sharp-residual} are checked over
\(\Q\); exactly \(308\) are zero.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-degree}]
Multiply \eqref{eq:sharp-residual} by \(n_j\) and sum over \(j\).
Using \(n_j\geq0\), \(t_i>0\), the inequalities \(R_in\geq\beta_i\),
and \eqref{eq:sharp-equalities}, one obtains
\[
 \begin{split}
 V+1=-\sum_jc_jn_j
 &=\sum_j r_jn_j+\sum_i t_iR_in
                         +\sum_{m=2}^{10}y_m(Q_m-1)\\
 &\geq\sum_i t_i\beta_i+\sum_{m=2}^{10}y_m(Q_m-1)
   =\frac{31802}{31801}.
 \end{split}
\]
Subtracting one proves \(V\geq1/31801\).
The coefficient of \(F\) is positive.
Equality in the final inequality therefore forces
\(R_Fn=66\), equivalently \(P_{11}=1\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{What is verified in the last finite calculation}

The data in Appendix~\ref{app:sharp-table} are a compact, exact
mathematical certificate. To verify them one performs the following
finite operations.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Regenerate \(\ccat_{10}\) from
Section~\ref{sec:cost}; compute its local correction functions from
\eqref{eq:local-correction} or \eqref{eq:floor-correction}.
\item For every local type compute \(c_j\) and \(u_j(m)\) through
degree \(211\).
\item Form the rational number \(r_j\) in
\eqref{eq:sharp-residual} and verify \(r_j\geq0\).
\item Verify the rational identity \eqref{eq:sharp-constant}.
\end{enumerate}

No optimization or geometric realization step occurs in this
verification. The program \texttt{efv\_verify\_sharp.py} also repeats
the analytic-tail checks and the finite local-cost checks.
The independently implemented \texttt{efv\_audit.py} recomputes
cyclic corrections by residue sums and fork corrections by full
rational matrix inversion before verifying the same certificate.
This second computation agrees on every column.

For clarity, the exact smallest positive residual is
\[
 \frac{560777345818934442730311715443416819}
      {3263535358870448119769812206994828584124080}.
\]
The number itself is not used elsewhere; it records the strict
positivity of all nonzero residuals.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:equality}
Equality implies that every local type used by \(X\) has \(r_j=0\),
and that each positively weighted section inequality is an equality.
These are necessary restrictions on an equality surface.
They do not determine the global surface, since local singularities
and anticanonical section dimensions alone need not determine
birational contraction data.
\end{remark}

\section{Sharpness and the weak del Pezzo case}\label{sec:conclusion}

\subsection{The extremal hypersurface}

Consider the hypersurface
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Totaro}
       X_{354}=\{w^2+z^3+xy^7+x^{17}yz=0\}
                         \subset\PP(11,49,118,177).
\end{equation}
The weights of \(x,y,z,w\) are \(11,49,118,177\).
All four monomials have weight \(354\):
\[
 2\cdot177=3\cdot118=11+7\cdot49=17\cdot11+49+118.
\]
Their weight sum is \(355\). Totaro's
\cite[Theorem 3.1]{Tot24} proves that this hypersurface is well-formed,
normal, klt, and Fano; it is not quasi-smooth.
His \cite[Theorem 6.1]{Tot24} gives
\[
                            \alpha(X_{354})=\frac{55}{6}>1.
\]
In particular it is exceptional. These assertions concern the
specific non-quasi-smooth hypersurface and do not follow merely
from a quasi-smoothness criterion.

Weighted adjunction and intersection give
\[
       -K_{X_{354}}=\OO_{X_{354}}(1),\qquad
       (-K_{X_{354}})^2
            =\frac{354}{11\cdot49\cdot118\cdot177}
            =\frac1{31801}.
\]
The anticanonical section ring is the graded coordinate ring
\[
 \C[x,y,z,w]/(w^2+z^3+xy^7+x^{17}yz).
\]
Its Hilbert series is
\[
                    \frac{1-t^{354}}
                    {(1-t^{11})(1-t^{49})(1-t^{118})(1-t^{177})}.
\]
In particular, the first positive degree is \(11\), and \(P_{11}=1\).
This agrees with the constraints forced by
Theorems~\ref{thm:focused} and \ref{thm:sharp-degree}.

\subsection{Proof of the main theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main} for del Pezzo surfaces]
If \(V>1/31801\), the desired inequality is immediate.
Otherwise Theorem~\ref{thm:focused} gives \(\mld(X)>1/11\)
and \(b\leq11\).
Proposition~\ref{prop:degree-eleven} gives \(b=11\).
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-degree} then yields \(V\geq1/31801\).
The surface \eqref{eq:Totaro} establishes sharpness.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:weak-model}
Let \(X\) be a klt weak del Pezzo surface and let
\(f:X\to Z\) be its anticanonical model.
Then \(Z\) is a klt del Pezzo surface,
\[
                     K_X=f^*K_Z,\qquad K_X^2=K_Z^2,
\]
and
\[
       H^0(X,\OO_X(-mK_X))=H^0(Z,\OO_Z(-mK_Z))
                         \quad\text{for every }m\geq0.
\]
If \(X\) is exceptional, then \(Z\) is exceptional.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The base-point-free theorem makes a sufficiently divisible multiple
of \(-K_X\) globally generated.
Since it is big, its anticanonical model gives a birational
contraction with connected fibers, and \(-K_X\) descends to an ample
\(\Q\)-Cartier divisor on \(Z\).
Pushing the canonical divisor forward identifies that divisor with
\(-K_Z\). In particular \(K_Z\) is \(\Q\)-Cartier.
The divisor \(K_X-f^*K_Z\) is exceptional and has zero intersection
with every contracted curve. Negative definiteness forces it to
vanish. Thus the contraction is crepant; the klt property and the
volume identity follow.

Use the common function field to represent sections by rational
functions. If \(\divi(h)-mK_Z\geq0\), its pullback is effective,
and equals \(\divi(h)-mK_X\).
The converse follows by pushforward. This proves the equality of
all reflexive section spaces, not only of Cartier multiples.

Finally, if \(D_Z\sim_{\Q}-K_Z\) is effective, then
\(D_X=f^*D_Z\sim_{\Q}-K_X\) is effective.
If \(X\) is exceptional, \((X,D_X)\) is klt, and
\[
                         K_X+D_X=f^*(K_Z+D_Z)
\]
makes \((Z,D_Z)\) klt.
Thus \(Z\) is exceptional.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Completion of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}]
Apply the del Pezzo case to \(Z\) and use
Lemma~\ref{lem:weak-model}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:equality}
If an exceptional klt del Pezzo surface has volume \(1/31801\),
then \(b=11\), \(P_{11}=1\), and the support model of its first
anticanonical member has log volume \(1/462\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
These are the conclusions of Proposition~\ref{prop:degree-eleven}
and Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-degree}.
\end{proof}

The corollary records the equality information needed to interpret
the proof. A classification of equality surfaces is a separate
problem, and is not asserted here.

\appendix
\section{Exact verification and certificate format}\label{app:verification}

\subsection{Running the verification}

All required files are included with the manuscript.
They use Python \(3.9\) or later and its standard library.
After compiling the TeX source, or extracting the files from the PDF
attachments or the separate verification archive, run:
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 efv_verify_nonvanishing.py
python3 efv_verify_sharp.py
python3 efv_audit.py
python3 efv_check_printed_table.py
\end{lstlisting}
The first two commands reconstruct the local catalogues and prove
the required finite assertions. The third uses a different
implementation of the local correction functions. The fourth
checks the coefficients printed in Appendix~\ref{app:sharp-table}
against the executable sharp certificate.
No earlier report or separately supplied mathematical data is needed.

The outputs distinguish the following assertions:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{p{41mm}|p{93mm}}
\toprule
calculation&exact assertion checked\\
\midrule
Eleven-vanishing local cost&
The tail starts at length \(535\);
\(1,694,986\) cyclic parameter tuples and all \(63\) fork bases
satisfy the stated inequalities.\\
Eleven-vanishing catalogue&
\(2063\) cyclic and \(51\) noncyclic types survive the exact filters.\\
Nonvanishing certificates&
One preliminary contradiction, \(33\) direct first-degree
contradictions, \(34\) contradictions in four integrality chains,
and \(18\) small-volume contradictions.\\
Ten-vanishing local cost&
The tail starts at length \(536\);
\(1,698,152\) cyclic parameter tuples and all \(63\) fork bases
satisfy the stated inequalities.\\
Sharp catalogue and dual&
\(2067\) cyclic and \(54\) noncyclic types; \(66\) positive row
coefficients and nine signed equality coefficients give
\(31802/31801\) as a lower bound for \(V+1\).\\
Independent arithmetic&
Residue sums for cyclic types and separately inverted fork matrices
give the same corrections and all \(2121\) sharp residuals.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All these computations were rerun for the present version.
The scripts write machine-readable verification summaries.
The archive includes these summaries and checksums of all source
and certificate files. Checksums identify the files; they are not
used as substitutes for verifying their mathematical contents.

\subsection{Exact generation of the local types}

The continued-fraction operation used throughout is
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
def hj(r, q):
    chain = []
    while q:
        e = (r + q - 1) // q
        chain.append(e)
        r, q = q, e*q - r
    return chain
\end{lstlisting}
For a cyclic type, the discrepancy numerators are computed from
\[
                p_0=r,\quad p_1=q+1,\quad
                p_{i+1}=e_ip_i-p_{i-1}.
\]
The discrepancies are \(p_i/r\), and \(p_{\ell+1}=r\).
Thus the strict test \(\mld>1/11\) is implemented as the integer
comparison \(11p_i>r\).
Normalization by \(q\leq q'\) removes exactly the reversal ambiguity.

For a fork, construct the diagonal entries from the central weight
and the three continued fractions, and put \(-1\) on every edge of
the matrix \(A\). Rational Gaussian elimination solves \(Ab=k\).
The local period is the least common multiple of the denominators
of the entries of \(b\). One period of
\eqref{eq:local-correction} then determines all degrees used by
the certificates.

For the infinite cyclic check, the program loops over exactly
\eqref{eq:param-a}--\eqref{eq:param-c} with the cutoff
\eqref{eq:n-range}. Its essential integer test is
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
assert cost_numerator > 0
if r >= 30:
    assert 100*cost_numerator >= 99*S*r*(r-1)
\end{lstlisting}
Here \texttt{cost\_numerator} means the integer on the right side of
\eqref{eq:integer-cost}. The tail inequalities are checked separately
before entering the loop. This is what makes a successful finite
loop a verification of the whole local assertion.

The local catalogue is never read from a data file.
The only numerical data read by the verification programs are
rational multipliers for linear identities.

\subsection{Rows in the nonvanishing certificates}

We describe the labels used in the JSON files. This makes it
possible to reconstruct every row without relying on an
optimization program or its conventions.
Fix a proposed first degree \(d\), put \(M=220\), and retain
the notation of \eqref{eq:u-definition}.
The convention is always \(A n\leq b_0\).

\begin{description}[leftmargin=24mm,style=nextline]
\item[\texttt{eq,0,\(\epsilon\)}]
The equality \(\sum_j u_j(2)n_j=2\), multiplied by
\(\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}\).
\item[\texttt{eq,\(a\),\(\epsilon\)}, \(a\geq1\)]
Put \(m=a+1\). This is
\(\sum_j(u_j(m+1)-2u_j(m)+u_j(m-1))n_j=1\),
multiplied by \(\epsilon\).
The allowed range is \(0\leq a<d-2\).
\item[\texttt{base,0}]
The row \(\sum_jc_jn_j\leq-1\).
\item[\texttt{base,1}]
The row \(\sum_j(\gamma_j-c_j)n_j\leq10\).
\item[\texttt{base,2}]
The row \(\sum_j(\mu_j-\frac43c_j)n_j\leq40/3\).
\item[\texttt{nonneg,\(m\)}]
The row \(P_m\geq0\), normalized as
\eqref{eq:nonneg-row}, with \(d\leq m\leq M\).
\item[\texttt{first,\(d\)}]
The row \(P_d\geq1\), normalized as \eqref{eq:first-row}.
\item[\texttt{shift,\(n\),\(d\)}]
The row \(P_{n+d}\geq P_n\), normalized as
\eqref{eq:shift-row}, with \(1\leq n\leq M-d\).
\item[\texttt{zero,\(m\),\(\epsilon\)}]
An already certified equality \(P_m=0\), normalized as
\[
 \epsilon\sum_j\frac{u_j(m)}{Q_m}n_j
                      \leq\epsilon\,\frac{Q_m-1}{Q_m}.
\]
\item[\texttt{nonzero,\(m\)}]
The temporary assumption \(P_m\geq1\), written
\(-\sum_j u_j(m)n_j/Q_m\leq-1\).
The checker allows only the degree being tested in that step of
an integrality chain.
\item[\texttt{volume\_ceiling,\(v\)}]
The row \(-\sum_jc_jn_j\leq1+v\); in the focused calculation,
\(v=1/31801\).
\item[\texttt{cost}]
The row \(\sum_j\cost_j^{(11)}n_j\leq U_{11}\).
\end{description}

All listed row multipliers are nonnegative rational numbers.
Signed equality coefficients are implemented by the sign
\(\epsilon\), not by allowing a negative multiplier on an inequality.
The separate field \texttt{cost\_correction} is an additional
nonnegative coefficient on the cost row.
Every constant is a string representing an integer or a rational
number; it is parsed by Python's \texttt{Fraction}.

To check a certificate the program accumulates coefficients
\(w_m,w_c,w_\gamma,w_\mu,w_{\rm cost}\) so that its column at a
type \(j\) is
\[
 \sum_{m=0}^{220}w_mu_j(m)+w_cc_j+w_\gamma\gamma_j
                   +w_\mu\mu_j+w_{\rm cost}\cost_j^{(11)}.
\]
It clears all denominators and checks the resulting column at
each regenerated type.
It also computes the combined right side and checks that it is
strictly negative.
The recorded minimum column and right side in each file are checked
against these freshly computed values.

The preliminary \(66\)-vanishing certificate has a simpler format:
\texttt{y} contains \(65\) signed equality multipliers,
\texttt{z} contains three nonnegative multipliers for the first three
global rows, and \texttt{delta} is the nonnegative cost multiplier.
Its equalities are \(u(2)=2\) followed by the \(64\) second differences
in \eqref{eq:zero-second-diff}.

\subsection{Integrality chains and logical dependencies}

In a file for first degree \(d\in\{30,31,32,34\}\), let
\((z_1,\ldots,z_s)\) be the list of forced zero degrees.
The verifier begins with an empty list of additional known zeros.
At step \(i\), it checks a certificate that assumes
\(P_{z_i}\geq1\), and permits only the zero rows with
degrees \(z_1,\ldots,z_{i-1}\).
After the contradiction has been established, it appends \(z_i\)
to the known-zero list.
The final certificate has no temporary nonzero assumption.
This ensures that each equality has been proved before it is used.

The \(18\) focused certificates require no such induction.
Their first degrees must be exactly \(12,\ldots,29\), their common
volume ceiling must be \(1/31801\), and their rows must satisfy the
same degree bounds. The finite systems retain all valid geometric
inequalities; relaxing strictness or allowing real multiplicities
cannot introduce an invalid exclusion.

\subsection{Independent local correction calculation}

For \(\frac1r(1,q)\), let \(q'\) be the inverse of \(q\bmod r\).
If \(i\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\) represents \(m(q+1)\bmod r\), a residue
expression for the correction is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:residue-a}
        a_x(m)=\frac1{2r}\sum_{\ell=1}^i
                       \bigl(r-1-2\overline{\ell q'}\bigr),
\end{equation}
where the bar is the least nonnegative residue modulo \(r\).
This follows from the same root-of-unity expression used in
Lemma~\ref{lem:cyclic-correction}, by expanding its numerator as
a finite geometric sum.
The independent checker uses \eqref{eq:residue-a} rather than the
floor sum. For forks it computes a full inverse matrix and evaluates
the quadratic form in \eqref{eq:local-correction} directly.
It then recomputes every sharp residual and the final constant.

These checks address arithmetic and implementation errors.
The geometric hypotheses behind the rows, the completeness of the
local parametrization, and the support-model argument remain the
mathematical parts of the proof.

\section{The sharp certificate in integers}\label{app:sharp-table}

The following tables give all coefficients used in
\eqref{eq:sharp-residual}--\eqref{eq:sharp-constant}.
They use the unscaled rows \(F,N_m,S_n\) of
Section~\ref{sec:sharp}.
Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:common-denominator}
                 D=163176767943522405988490610349741429206204000.
\end{equation}
The table entry \(z_i\) means \(t_i=z_i/D\); the signed entry \(v_m\)
means \(y_m=v_m/D\).
All \(z_i\) are strictly positive.
The coefficient \(v_5=0\) is retained to keep the equality degrees
\(2,\ldots,10\) consecutive.

The exact assertions are
\begin{align}
 -Dc_j-\sum_i z_iR_{ij}-\sum_{m=2}^{10}v_mu_j(m)&\geq0
                                      &&(j\in\ccat_{10}),\label{eq:integer-sharp}\\
 \sum_i z_i\beta_i+\sum_{m=2}^{10}v_m(Q_m-1)
              &=D\,\frac{31802}{31801}.\label{eq:integer-sharp-rhs}
\end{align}
The right side is an integer. The attached
\texttt{efv\_check\_printed\_table.py} confirms that these tables are
exactly the normalized form of the rational certificate and checks
both identities on the regenerated catalogue.

\section{A small arithmetic control example}\label{app:control}

The known extremal surface provides an additional check of the
normalization and the signs.
Its local numerical basket is
\[
             \left\{\frac1{49}(1,17),\
                         \frac1{59}(1,42),\
                         \frac1{22}(1,13)\right\}.
\]
Inserting these three local types in the formulas of
Sections~\ref{sec:global} and \ref{sec:local} gives
\[
 \rho=2,\qquad V=\frac1{31801},\qquad
 P_1=\cdots=P_{10}=0,\qquad P_{11}=1.
\]
Every positively weighted row of the sharp certificate is an
equality on these data, and all three local residuals vanish.
The control calculation is included in
\texttt{efv\_check\_printed\_table.py}.
It is a normalization check, not an input to the proof of the
lower bound: the proof uses only the complete local catalogue and
the inequalities valid for an arbitrary exceptional surface.
